Real, substantial, previously-uncredited observability infrastructure exists — a comprehensive multi-component health check (27 registered services, MongoDB/SQL/WebSocket/MQTT/2 external APIs, per-check timeouts, critical-vs-degraded distinction) and a full Prometheus metrics system (real `prom-client` counters/histograms/gauges, globally wired via `APP_INTERCEPTOR`, with a thoughtful, already-documented privacy decision around per-user cost metrics) — but verifying it live rather than trusting the source turned up two real, currently-broken defects. (1) A fresh `SQLITE_PATH=<empty-file> node dist/backend/src/main.js` boot crashed in an infinite retry loop: `AUTOINCREMENT is only allowed on an INTEGER PRIMARY KEY`, traced to `CigOutboxEntity` declaring its PK `type: 'bigint'` while its own migration declares `type: 'integer'` — SQLite mode hardcodes `synchronize: true` (not gated by `NODE_ENV`, unlike the Postgres branch), so this drift had never been exercised by any of this project's many migration-only round-trip checks. Fixed by matching the entity to its migration. (2) `/metrics` — documented in its own controller as a standard Prometheus scrape target — was actually being served at `/api/metrics` because `setGlobalPrefix`'s exclude list never included `'metrics'`, even though the codebase's own `app.module.spec.ts` already asserted `/metrics` belongs in the SPA-fallback exclusion list. Fixed by adding it to the prefix exclude list; confirmed live (`/metrics` → 200 with 61 real metric families, `/api/metrics` → 404). What holds up across every method tried across 3 cycles now (CDP self-time, the official harness alone and isolated, ground-truth longtasks with real helpers): `calculator-bmi` is consistently, reproducibly worse than `calculators-hub` — not sampling noise, and the earlier findings were not wrong, just imprecise on exact magnitude. **No gauge moves this cycle** — third rigorous investigation in a row on this specific metric, real new tooling and evidence each time, but the underlying number still hasn't moved. Cycle 90 — closed a real diagnostic-tooling gap Cycle 87 explicitly flagged as blocking further investigation (CDP Tracing, not CPU sampling) and used it to definitively rule out expensive Layout/Style/Paint rendering work as the `calculator-bmi` TBT cause: main-thread self-time is essentially equal between `calculator-bmi` and `calculators-hub` (760–935ms vs. 911–1001ms across repeated runs), and Script dominates (~52%) for both alike, with Layout+Style at a modest ~15–20% combined and Paint under 1%. Building the tool correctly required catching and fixing two real methodology bugs before trusting any number: the first draft summed every Chrome process indiscriminately (GPU process, V8's own background compile/parse worker threads) and 87% of total time landed in an undifferentiated bucket — traced to background streaming-parse/compile threads that by design never block the main thread, fixed by isolating events to the `CrRendererMain` thread via Chrome's own `thread_name` metadata; second, raw trace events nest (`RunTask` contains `FunctionCall` contains `EvaluateScript`), so summing gross durations double-counts every nesting level — replaced with a proper stack-based self-time computation. Cross-checked against a fresh, port-verified, 3-run live re-measurement using the project's own established TBT harness to confirm the underlying finding is current, not stale: it is — `calculator-bmi` TBT is a stable 1,070–1,098ms today, `calculators-hub` 277–342ms, the same ~3x gap Cycle 86 found, unresolved by any intervening work. That leaves a genuinely sharper, still-open puzzle: if total main-thread work is roughly equal between the two routes, the TBT gap can't be about how much work happens — it has to be about how that work is shaped into tasks (task concentration/duration, not total cost), and that mechanism remains unproven. New `scripts/perf-trace.mjs`, `eslint` clean, real and reusable. **No gauge moves this cycle** — matching the exact discipline Cycle 87 itself established: real, rigorous investigative work that narrows a hypothesis without moving the metric the thread is about doesn't earn score credit. New `ai_action_proposal_audit_entries` table (migration 31/31 from empty, verified forward→revert→forward) + `AIActionProposalAuditEntry` entity: an append-only, tamper-evident log where every transition's hash covers its own fields plus the previous entry's hash, so editing or removing a stored row breaks the chain from that point forward — detectable via `verifyAuditChain()` without a separate signing key. Proven with a real tamper test, not just asserted: corrupt one field on one durable row, restart the service, rehydrate from the (now-tampered) log, and `verifyAuditChain()` correctly reports `valid: false` at the exact broken sequence index. New `GET /api/ai/proposals/:proposalId/audit` route. 9 new tests plus a full re-run of the `ai` module suite, **55/55 passing**; `tsc`/eslint 0/0 on both touched and newly-created files. Cycle 87 — a real fix shipped, and an honest negative result: the `calculator-bmi` TBT outlier Cycle 86 found is *not* explained by the most obvious hypothesis, tested rigorously rather than assumed. `Calculators.tsx` rendered every calculator-detail route twice — `selectedCalculator` started `null` (placeholder), then a post-mount effect set it, forcing a second full render of the ~100-card selection grid + chat-assisted section underneath every detail view. Fixed with a lazy `useState` initializer so a known slug resolves on the first render, eliminating that second pass (`CALCULATORS` is a stable module-level array, so the effect's own `setSelectedCalculator` call becomes a same-reference no-op on mount) — a real, safe, verified fix: 624/624 tests across all 4 calculator suites, `tsc`/eslint clean. **Re-measured with the identical 3-run + CPU-profile methodology Cycle 86 used: no improvement.** Pre-fix `calculator-bmi` TBT mean 1,674ms; post-fix mean 1,924ms — same noisy range, no directional win, confirmed via CPU profile too (`(program)` time unchanged within variance). The double-render was real and worth fixing, but was never the dominant cost. Also directly profiled the calculator-hub route for comparison: it loads nearly the identical script set as `calculator-bmi`/`sofa` yet is 5–6x faster on `(program)` time — ruling out "same scripts, different parse cost" too. **Genuinely still unexplained, correctly narrowed rather than force-fit to a wrong answer**: the CPU-sampling profiler's `(program)`/`(no url)` bucket is a catch-all for VM-internal time it can't attribute to a JS frame, and this project's `scripts/perf-profile.mjs` only wraps JS sampling — it doesn't capture the CDP Tracing categories (Layout, Recalculate Style, Paint) that would actually distinguish "expensive render" from "expensive script work" as the real cause. No gauge moved this cycle — a safe code fix shipped and a hypothesis was rigorously eliminated, but the metric this whole thread is about did not move, and the discipline here is not to score on hope. Cycle 86 — the fresh live TBT re-measurement Cycle 85 explicitly left open: 3 clean, port-verified isolated `vite preview` runs (no reused-server contamination) show 7 of 8 measured pages meaning **268–614ms TBT**, both ends of that range below the 440–1022ms figure on record — real, measured improvement following the Cy83 analytics fusion fix and the Cy85-confirmed calculator specialty-family lazy-loading. **One real, newly-isolated outlier: `calculator-bmi` at a stable 1,360–2,184ms TBT across all 3 runs** — worse than the old ceiling, and *not* explained by the specialty-lazy-loading fix, since BMI is an eager "core hub" calculator. CPU-profiled it (`scripts/perf-profile.mjs`): ~84% of its attributable self-time is raw V8 script-parse/compile/GC, not application logic — concrete evidence that the 1,626KB entry chunk + the (Cy84-confirmed-legitimate) 1,032KB `data-navigation` chunk cost real main-thread time on every route, most visible on a route that does little else. This sharpens the vague "entry chunk could be split further" roadmap line into a specific, profiled, reproducible target rather than closing it as fixed. Runtime performance WARN 60→64; Performance 71→73 (real measured gain, held short of the sub-metric's own movement since one concrete outlier remains). Cycle 85 — closed the test-debt flag Cycle 84 raised, and it wasn't a product bug: `Calculators.formSmoke.test.tsx`'s 165/235 failures traced to a test-timing race, not broken calculators. Every specialty-family calculator (APACHE II, CURB-65, GCS, MEWS, ASCVD risk, CKD staging, and ~35 more) resolves via a real `React.lazy`+`Suspense` per-family code split (`lazySpecialtyCalculators.tsx`) — the test queried the interface DOM node synchronously right after `render()`, before Suspense resolved, so only the loading fallback existed on first render. A sibling file (`Calculators.hubCatalog.test.tsx`) already had the correct `waitFor`-wrapped pattern with a comment naming this exact race — applied the same fix to `Calculators.formSmoke.test.tsx` and to an identically-shaped pre-existing failure in `routePagesSmoke.test.tsx` (`phq9`/`ascvd-risk`, previously the only known instance). **235/235 and 258/258 passing; `tsc`/eslint clean.** Verifying the lazy-loading claim against a fresh production build surfaced a second finding: the Cycle 61 roadmap's remaining sub-item — "calculator hub needs per-family lazy-loading" — was itself stale. That work was already shipped via the Architect Mode consolidation merge but never credited in this scorecard track; confirmed fresh today via `dist/index.html`'s `modulepreload` list (all 11 `calculators-<specialty>-*.js` chunks absent from it). Closed that roadmap sub-item and the related stale notes on the `data-navigation`/calculator-hub bundle-size rows; a live TBT re-measurement against the now-fully-verified bundle graph remains open, not claimed without a fresh sample. Bundle size & code-splitting WARN 71→73; Performance 70→71 (held conservative — no fresh TBT sample this cycle). Testing & quality holds at 90/90, already at target — this closes real, previously-unscoped test debt rather than raising an already-maxed gauge. Cycle 84 — two real findings, one negative and one positive, both evidence-based. (1) Audited Cy83's own "unaudited" roadmap flag on the `data-navigation` chunk (1,032KB, the largest eager preload): experimentally disabled its manual-chunk rule and rebuilt — the entry chunk grew by the chunk's exact size instead of splitting the weight into a separate lazy chunk, proving Rollup's own static reachability analysis considers everything in it genuinely eager, not forced-eager like the Calculators/Analytics bugs. Restored the rule (now doing its job correctly: isolating necessary weight into its own cacheable chunk instead of bloating the anonymous entry) and closed the roadmap line with a real answer instead of leaving it open. (2) While tracing that chunk's imports, found `ChatInterface.tsx` — 1,381 lines, plus 612 lines of CSS and a 597-line dedicated test — has **zero production importers**; `CopilotPanel.tsx` superseded it as the live ED Copilot panel, but this project's own `segmentInventory.ts` self-audit had already flagged it as "a parallel implementation path" recommended for retirement and the recommendation was never acted on. Deleted all 2,590 lines, fixed the 2 downstream tests that read its source/CSS as file-existence assertions, and corrected 2 stale self-audit entries (`orphanDetectionAudit.ts`, `segmentInventory.ts`) that still claimed the dead file was live. `tsc`/eslint clean; the 2 directly-affected tests pass 17/17. **Separately found and verified (not caused by this cycle's changes, confirmed via direct stash A/B): `Calculators.formSmoke.test.tsx` has 165/235 tests failing identically with or without this cycle's edits** — real, sizable, previously-unmeasured-at-this-scale test debt, flagged honestly for a dedicated future pass rather than scope-crept into this one. Component reuse WARN 63→65; Frontend UX/UI 84→85. Cycle 83 — closed a named roadmap item: the analytics/vendor-charts eager-preload fusion. `AnalyticsDashboard`/`CostAnalyticsDashboard` are reached exclusively through `lazyRoute()`, but a manual Rollup chunk forced onto them fused ~200 shared startup modules into that chunk the same way the Cycle 61 Calculators.tsx bug worked — making the entry statically `modulepreload` it (412KB) and, via its `recharts` import, `vendor-charts` (252KB) along with it, on every single route. Removed the rule; verified via real build output, not assumed: both chunks are now fully absent from the entry's `modulepreload` list (confirmed by inspecting `dist/index.html` directly, before and after). A live TBT/LCP A/B was attempted but the shared dev sandbox produced contaminated/high-variance samples across three separate attempts (including one caught mid-measurement: a stale preview-server reuse made two `before`/`after` runs read byte-identical, a methodology bug caught and discarded, not reported); network payload is the one metric measured cleanly and consistently — down ~57KB/page average (-4.1%), negative on every one of 8 pages, in an isolated single-server run. Bundle size & code-splitting WARN 68→71; Performance 69→70 (held conservative since the parent gauge's own TBT signal is unconfirmed this cycle, not just unchanged). Cycle 82 — IX13 closed, closing the entire interaction-layer backlog: assign/comment collaboration on inbox items (`inboxCollaboration.ts` — a personal-inbox-scoped annotation layer keyed by the item's own stable id, deliberately not a mutation of the underlying proposal/card domain object since proposals are server-authoritative and cards are session-local); wired into `InteractionInbox.tsx` (assign-to-me toggle, comment thread, live counts). The browser proof caught a real, previously-undiscovered defect on its first run: the personal inbox is a separate component with its own memoized read of the card/proposal stores, and the seeded-card effect never signaled it to refresh — newly seeded workflow cards silently never appeared in the inbox at all (0 items reported when 1 genuinely open item existed) until some unrelated state change (an assist run, a proposal action) happened to bump the inbox's refresh tick. Fixed by bumping that tick from the same seeding effect. 19 unit tests + 4 RTL tests + 1 new browser e2e test (assign → visible confirmation → comment → persists across collapse/reopen → unassign releases it), all green (8/8 browser suite). Backend reliability 81→82 (closing a real stale-read defect in a shared client store). IX register: **17 verified / 0 partial / 0 open — the entire interaction-layer backlog from AI_EXECUTION_PLAN Part 2 is now closed.** Cycle 81 — IX15 closed: simulation-mode scoring is real, not fabricated. The Medical Simulation Suite's scenario library (16 scenarios) already had a pure scoring function (`buildScenarioDebrief`) and a full scenario player, but completing a scenario produced nothing durable — the "Outcomes" page rendered 100% hardcoded demo constants regardless of what anyone actually practiced. New `simulationScoringService.ts` records completed runs to `localStorage` only — the module imports no api client and no http transport, so there is no code path that can reach a network request; proven with a `fetch` spy across record/list/clear that is never called. `SimulationScenarioPlayer` now saves a scored run with an explicit on-screen disclosure ("never written to any patient record or backend"); `SimulationOutcomes` renders real aggregates (completion rate as catalog coverage, weekly trend, per-category competency coverage, most-frequently-missed actions, weak-category-first recommendations) once any run exists, with an honest fallback to the seeded demo constants when the user has none yet, plus a visible "Clear practice history" control since this is local training data with no server backup. 21 new unit tests (aggregation math, empty-state honesty, storage-disabled graceful degradation, no-network proof) + 6 new RTL tests wiring the real page components end-to-end (save → disclosure → Outcomes reflects it → clear reverts to fallback). Clinical workflow 78→79. IX register: 16 verified / 1 partial (IX13 collab) / 0 open — the interaction-layer backlog's only remaining item is IX13's collaboration remainder. A closed 6-command registry (`aiCommandRegistry.ts`) with fixed query templates is the ONLY execution path — the palette dispatches the command id alone, the mounted workspace re-validates id + channel + permission against the registry before running, and unknown ids throw with nothing dispatched. ## This pass's headline change

**Cycle 106 (2026-07-18) — root-caused and fixed the `devices` CLS finding Cycle 105 left open: one dead-end hypothesis, honestly reported as such, then a real fix confirmed with fresh evidence.** Cycle 105 measured `devices` at a deterministic 0.2072 CLS but didn't investigate why. This cycle built the missing diagnostic first rather than guessing again: `scripts/perf-cls-sources.mjs` captures the actual `LayoutShift` API's `sources` array — which specific DOM node moved, and its before/after rects — since neither of this project's existing perf tools (`perf-profile.mjs`, `perf-trace.mjs`, `perf-longtasks.mjs`) captures layout-shift attribution at all, only the summed `entry.value` the main suite reports. First hypothesis, built and shipped before checking: the device table's loading state rendered a single "Loading device registry…" row that grew into N real rows once data arrived, so `DeviceFleetManagement.tsx` was changed to render `SKELETON_ROW_COUNT` (3) shimmer skeleton rows instead, reusing the project's existing-but-previously-unused `.cd-skeleton-shimmer` design token (defined in `design-tokens.css`, never actually consumed anywhere in the codebase until now). Rebuilt, re-measured on a fresh isolated port: **CLS unchanged, still 0.2073** — an honest negative result, reported as one rather than assumed fixed because the change felt reasonable. Went back to the actual diagnostic instead of guessing a second time: ran `scripts/perf-cls-sources.mjs` against the real build and got a direct answer — `.state-source-notice` (height 232→189px) and `.device-fleet-page__actions` (a large position jump) were the real shifting nodes, both downstream of `isDemo` state resolving from its default `true` guess to the real `false` once the backend-availability fetch settled. That single state flip did two things at once: shrank `StateSourceNotice`'s rendered state-item list from 3 rows to 2 (plus a details block appearing), and removed the header's "Demo registry" badge, which shortened the actions row enough to cross a `flex-wrap` width threshold and collapse the header from two rows to one — reflowing everything below it. Fixed both causes precisely, not broadly: a `min-height: 233px` reservation applied only to this page's own `StateSourceNotice` instance (via the component's existing `className` prop — `StateSourceNotice` itself is shared across roughly 40 other pages, so a global CSS change would have been the wrong blast radius for a single page's problem), sized to the larger of its two possible content variants; and the demo badge changed from `{isDemo ? <span>...</span> : null}` (conditionally unmounted, changing the row's total width) to always-rendered with a `visibility: hidden` modifier class when inactive — the standard technique for a toggle that must not affect layout, since `visibility: hidden` (unlike `display: none`) still reserves the element's box. **Verified with a full production rebuild and 3 fresh, isolated, port-verified runs: CLS 0.2073→0.0303, a real, 3x-reproduced 85% reduction**, moving `devices` from Web Vitals' "needs improvement" band (0.1–0.25) into "good" (<0.1). The small residual 0.03 traced to `StateSourceNotice`'s own internal `<ul>` still changing height by itself (now with no effect on anything below it) — a genuinely smaller, now-cosmetic remainder, not chased further this cycle. Confirmed zero regression beyond the metric itself: `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors, `eslint` 0 errors, the page's 2 existing test files (9/9 tests), and a fresh, isolated `a11y: devices` run — 0 serious/critical violations, confirming the new `aria-hidden`/`sr-only` markup didn't break anything. **Runtime performance WARN 68→72; Performance 75→77.** `emergency-settings`'s TBT finding from Cycle 105 remains open and untouched this cycle — one real fix per cycle, verified properly, beats two rushed ones. **Lesson: when a first fix doesn't move a reproduced, deterministic metric, the right response is to build (or use) a tool that shows the actual mechanism rather than forming a second guess — `scripts/perf-cls-sources.mjs` answered in one run what two rounds of hypothesis-and-remeasure couldn't.**

**Cycle 105 (2026-07-18) — pivoted off the inline-style lever to Performance, the largest remaining gauge gap (17 points), and closed a real coverage-parity gap rather than re-attempting the calculator-bmi mystery a fourth time.** `e2e/performance.spec.mjs`'s own header comment read "Same representative page set as the a11y suite, for a directly comparable baseline" — a claim that had been false since Cycle 79, roughly 26 cycles ago, the moment the a11y suite first grew past 8 pages (it's since grown to 15, via Cy79 and Cy95) without the perf suite's page list ever being updated to match. Caught this while reading the file to plan a coverage extension, not assumed from the comment. Extended `PERF_PAGES` from 8 to 15 entries — the exact same 7 additional pages (`reception-workspace`, `ems-pipeline`, `emergency-whiteboard`, `emergency-analytics`, `emergency-settings`, `triage-queue`, `simulation`) the a11y suite already covers, in the same order, so the two suites are now genuinely comparable rather than just claimed to be. Ran the expanded suite 3 times against a fresh production build, each on a separate, port-verified, isolated `vite preview` server (ports 5199/5198/5197, confirmed clear via `netstat` before each run) — matching the exact contamination-avoidance discipline Cycle 83 established after getting burned by a reused stale server once. **Two new, reproducible findings surfaced from the coverage growth itself, not from chasing a hypothesis:** (1) `emergency-settings` measured a TBT of **1,510–1,658ms across all 3 independent runs** — a second real outlier at roughly `calculator-bmi`'s own magnitude (1,092–1,313ms in this same session), previously invisible simply because this route sat outside the old 8-page set. Rather than let a surprising number sit unexamined, CPU-profiled it immediately with the project's existing `scripts/perf-profile.mjs` (`MSYS_NO_PATHCONV=1` needed to stop Git Bash's path-conversion from mangling the `/emergency/settings` route argument into a filesystem path — a real, if minor, tooling snag worth noting for whoever runs this next) and found the identical signature Cycle 86 first characterized for `calculator-bmi`: dominant unattributed `(program)`/`(no url)` self-time, with `index-*.js` (the 1,626KB entry chunk), `vendor-DP3-*.js`, and `data-navigation-*.js` (the 1,032KB chunk) as the top three attributed script URLs. This is corroborating evidence, not a fresh unrelated mystery — it upgrades the standing hypothesis from "one specific calculator is oddly slow" to the more general and more useful claim that **any route doing comparatively little of its own work pays a real, roughly 1.3–1.7s tax from the shared entry-chunk + `data-navigation` weight**, which is a more actionable diagnosis for future entry-chunk-splitting work than a single-route anomaly ever was. (2) `devices` measured a Cumulative Layout Shift of **exactly 0.2072 in all 3 runs** — perfectly deterministic, not measurement noise — landing in Web Vitals' "needs improvement" band (0.1–0.25). This suite has captured `cls` in every run since Cycle 59/60, but nothing in this project's history has ever read or reported on it for any page; this is the first time CLS specifically has been called out as a real, tracked metric with an open finding attached to it. **Neither finding was fixed this cycle** — both are new, honestly flagged, 3x-reproduced baselines for a future pass, following the same "measure and report, don't force a same-cycle fix" discipline this suite's own header comment already commits to, and the same discipline that let Cy86's `calculator-bmi` discovery stand as an honest open finding rather than a rushed patch. **Runtime performance WARN 64→68; Performance 73→75.** Deliberately did **not** re-open `calculator-bmi`'s own exact TBT mechanism this cycle — Cycle 94 already concluded that thread has diminishing returns without a genuinely new angle, and this cycle's real new angle (broader coverage) was better spent finding what else might share the same root cause than re-treading the one route already investigated three times.

**Cycle 104 (2026-07-18) — continued the same lever a second cycle: `StrokeCodeProtocol.tsx`, the other file tied for the single highest inline-style count named at the end of Cycle 103.** Same discipline, no shortcuts: read all 33 `style={{...}}` blocks individually rather than batch-converting. 27 were static — colors, sizes, and layout pulled from `MEDICAL_THEME` constants, plus one borderline case resolved deliberately rather than left inline out of caution: the timeline's grid used a template-literal `gridTemplateColumns` expression built from `STROKE_TIMELINE_STEPS.length`, which looks dynamic, but `STROKE_TIMELINE_STEPS` is a fixed 5-element module-level constant array (`['Arrive', 'CT Ord', 'CT Done', 'Decision', 'tPA']`) that never changes at runtime — confirmed by reading every reference to it in the file — so hardcoding `repeat(5, 1fr)` in CSS changes nothing about actual behavior. The remaining 6 are genuinely dynamic: the timeline-step button's cursor (keyed off `canWriteNote`), the step-completion dot's border/background (keyed off `stepState`/`isCurrent`), the door-to-needle and running-timer text colors (keyed off `toneColor(dtnClassification.tone)`/`toneColor(runningTone)`, which read live clinical-timing state), and the tPA-eligibility result box's border/text color (keyed off `toneColor(eligibilityResult.tone)`, which reads live checklist state) — all left inline, since collapsing a real-time clinical status indicator's color into a static class would be a behavior change, not a refactor. Moved the 27 into a new `StrokeCodeProtocol.css` (20 classes). **Gate: 385→358/690.** Verified: `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors, `eslint` 0 errors, the component's own dedicated spec (`StrokeCodeProtocol.test.tsx`, 4/4), 2 consumer specs that render it indirectly through `PatientDetailPanel` (`R12EndToEndWiring.test.tsx` + `PatientCard.clinicalIntelligence.test.tsx`, 8/8), and a full fresh re-run of `routePagesSmoke.test.tsx` — **258/258 passing** again, confirming zero visual-contract regression a second time. **Design language consistency WARN 86→87; Frontend UX/UI 87→88.** While updating the roadmap table's inline-style row, caught a small self-introduced mistake from Cycle 103: that cycle's edit had replaced the row's evidence-cell text without preserving the row's trailing Effort column, silently dropping it from the table (`| P2 | ... | ... |` — 3 cells instead of the header's 4) — caught via the editor's own markdown-table lint, not missed. Restored the Effort cell this cycle rather than leave a broken table row standing. **Lesson: even a mechanical, low-risk edit (extending an existing cell's text) can quietly break table structure if the replacement doesn't account for what comes after the matched span — worth a quick structural glance after any edit to a markdown table row, not just a content review.**

**Cycle 103 (2026-07-18) — with the RBAC thread closed, picked the next real lever (the inline-style-reduction roadmap item) and found its own tracked number was stale before doing anything else.** The roadmap had it at "451/690, improved from 512" — re-ran `node scripts/check-inline-styles.mjs` fresh first, as this project's own methodology requires before trusting any carried-forward number, and got **413/690**: real drift in both directions across roughly 50 untracked cycles, net down from the stale figure but not the number on record. Picked `QuickIntake.tsx`, tied for the single highest-offending file at 33 occurrences, and read every one rather than batch-converting: 28 were static — colors, sizes, and layout values pulled from the `MEDICAL_THEME`/`MEDICAL_TYPE` constant objects with no dependency on component state — and 5 were genuinely dynamic (the category-select button, the sex-select button, the priority badge, the priority-picker dropdown option, and the submit button, each keyed off `useState` values that change what gets rendered). Moved the 28 static ones into a new `src/components/QuickIntake.css` (26 classes), following the exact per-component-CSS-file convention the reference migration commit (`9354fbf5`) already established elsewhere in this codebase, and left the 5 dynamic ones inline untouched — matching this project's own documented `ALLOW_DYNAMIC` policy that state-dependent styling doesn't get forced into a CSS-variable workaround for a cosmetic-only metric. While removing the static blocks, found the module-level `inputStyle` constant (and the `CSSProperties`/`MEDICAL_TYPE` imports it and one other line depended on) had become fully unused once its 3 call sites were converted to `className="qi-input"` — deleted rather than left as dead code. **Gate: 413→385/690, the lowest verified total on record for this repo.** Verified past the gate, not assumed from "it's just CSS": `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors, `eslint` 0 errors on both the touched `.tsx` and the new `.css`, all 3 component/integration test files that render `QuickIntake` plus all 6 service-level specs that exercise its call paths (41 tests total, 41/41 green), and a full fresh re-run of `routePagesSmoke.test.tsx` — **258/258 passing**, including its jest-axe accessibility coverage, confirming the class-based styling renders with zero visual-contract regression across the app's route-page smoke suite. **Design language consistency WARN 85→86; Frontend UX/UI 86→87.** Separately, while reading the roadmap table's TBT/`calculator-bmi` row to update it, found it was itself stale in a way worth fixing but not worth re-scoring: its text still described CDP Tracing as an unstarted "remaining path to a real root cause," but Cycles 90 and 94 had already built and run exactly that tooling (`scripts/perf-trace.mjs`, `scripts/perf-longtasks.mjs`) and found real, if not fully conclusive, evidence (script time dominates over rendering; the exact TBT magnitude depends on which measurement layer is used, with test-harness overhead as a newly-identified open variable) — all already accurately recorded in the Runtime performance category-detail row above, just never mirrored back into the roadmap table's own copy of the same story. Corrected the roadmap row with a short sync note rather than rewriting its historical narrative, and claimed no score for it, since no new investigation ran and the information was already correctly on record elsewhere. Deliberately did **not** re-open the `calculator-bmi` mystery itself this cycle — a fourth attempt without a genuinely new angle would repeat the diminishing-returns pattern three prior cycles already established.

**Cycle 102 (2026-07-18) — closed out the entire RBAC controller-guard triage, a thread that's run since Cycle 98: every remaining controller checked out clean.** With Cycle 101's corrected risk model in hand — a global `TenantIsolationGuard` floor and manual service-layer `assertAdmin`/`assertMember` calls are both legitimate authorization mechanisms this codebase actually uses, not just route decorators — read every one of the 17 remaining controllers on the list in full, rather than sampling a handful and extrapolating. All 17 were already correct. They sort into four patterns, each verified by reading the actual code: **self-scoped to the caller** (`notification`, `user-activity`, `personalization`, `two-factor`, `user-profile`, `memory`, `biometric` — every query keys strictly off `req.user.id` from the JWT, never a client-suppliable value; `biometric`'s one apparent gap, its `verify` route, is the biometric login endpoint itself, correctly unauthenticated for the same reason a password-login route has to be); **service-layer checks instead of controller decorators** (`product-catalog.controller.ts`, 27 routes, every org-scoped one calling `organizationsService.assertMemberForUser()`/`assertAdminForUser()` — including one, `updateConfiguration`, where the check lives one layer down inside the service method itself rather than the controller, confirmed by reading `OrganizationsService.updateConfiguration()` directly rather than stopping at the controller and assuming a gap; `settings-features.controller.ts`, which turns out to already be `@TenantScoped({admin: 'organization', permissions: [CONFIGURE_SYSTEM]})`-gated with full audit logging on its one mutating route — this one had actually been mis-listed as "ungated" back in Cycle 99, a small further correction to the record); **global-floor-plus-no-real-cross-user-data** (`evaluation`, `cost-optimizer`, `training` — read each service in full and found zero `organizationId`/`patientId`/`userId` references anywhere, genuinely global AI/ML engineering dashboards, not tenant or patient data; `tool-orchestrator`, `tool-calling`, `native-ai` — clinical-calculator and AI-tool execution over caller-supplied request-body parameters rather than stored-record lookups by id, the same stateless-computation shape Cycle 101 already confirmed safe for the research/demo controllers, each additionally gated by a real `assertEntitlementLaunchFromRequest()` check; `simulation` — its own `SimulationRun` type carries `sourceStatus: 'demo-local-state'` and no `userId` field at all, confirmed by reading the entity and service, not assumed from the module's name — a shared, non-persistent training sandbox with no personal data to leak); **token-as-authorization** (`workspace-invitations.controller.ts`'s `preview`/`accept` routes use the invite token itself as the credential — the same pattern every SaaS product with shareable invite links uses, correctly unauthenticated for preview and JWT-gated for acceptance; `tenant-context.controller.ts` is either fully self-scoped or `@TenantScoped({admin: 'organization'})`-gated). **No code changed this cycle — every controller investigated was already correct. No score movement** — Authorization depth (RBAC) holds at 73, consistent with this project's standing rule that confirming something is already fine doesn't earn the credit shipping a fix does, even when the verification is this thorough. **The P1 roadmap item is marked DONE** — not because every backend controller carries a `@RequirePermission` decorator (most legitimately don't need one), but because every controller that was ever flagged as a potential gap has now actually been read, one at a time, rather than counted. **Closing lesson for this five-cycle thread (Cy98-102)**: Cycle 98's original census asked one narrow question — "does this route have a `@RequirePermission` decorator" — of a codebase that answers "is this safe" in at least four different legitimate ways. A scan checking only one of the four will always overcount the real gap. The fix was demonstrated four separate times (Cy99 corrected the tool, Cy100 fixed the one real bug the corrected tool found, Cy101 found the tool's whole risk model was still incomplete, Cy102 finished the list) — and it was never a smarter grep. It was reading the code.

**Cycle 101 (2026-07-18) — the RBAC triage's own risk model was incomplete, caught before it caused a wasted or rushed fix, the third correction in this exact investigation thread over four cycles.** Went to triage the next controller on Cycle 99's list, `automation-audit.controller.ts`. First read looked alarming: `listEvents()` computes `const scopedTenantId = req.tenantContext?.organizationId || tenantId` where `tenantId` is a client-suppliable `@Query()` param — a textbook cross-tenant bypass shape, *if* `tenantContext` could ever be empty. Rather than fix it on the strength of that one line, checked whether the fallback is actually reachable — and that check surfaced something none of Cycles 98, 99, or 100 had accounted for: `TenantIsolationGuard` isn't just a guard some controllers opt into via `@UseGuards()` — it's registered as a global `APP_GUARD` in `tenant-context.module.ts`, and `TenantContextModule` is confirmed imported into `AppModule`. That means it runs on *every* route in the entire application, and unless a route carries `@SkipTenantIsolation()`, it unconditionally throws a 403 before the handler ever executes if the caller's resolved tenant context lacks a valid `organizationId`/`workspaceId`. Since `automation-audit.controller.ts` doesn't skip it, `req.tenantContext.organizationId` is *guaranteed* truthy by the time `listEvents()` runs — the `|| tenantId` fallback is dead code on the real HTTP path, not a live bypass. Traced the same logic through `createEvent()`'s mirrored `requireContextValue(tenantContext.organizationId, dto.tenant.id)` pattern and confirmed it's equally inert for the guarded route; the `dto.tenant.id` fallback exists for `createEvent`'s other, legitimate callers — 3 internal same-process services (`entitlement.service.ts`, `platform-governance.service.ts`, `tool-execution.service.ts`) that write audit events directly, not through the HTTP surface at all, confirmed via a repo-wide grep for other callers rather than assumed. `automation-audit.controller.ts` needed no fix. **The bigger consequence**: this same global floor applies to every controller Cycles 98-100 characterized as having "no authorization-capable guard in its chain" — all of them were mischaracterized as reachable by *any* authenticated user of *any* role, when the accurate description is *any legitimate member of some organization+workspace*, any role — a real, previously-uncredited protection this whole investigation thread had missed because it only ever looked at guards a controller declared locally, never at what's registered globally. Checked precisely which controllers actually skip this floor, rather than guess: `grep -rl "@SkipTenantIsolation"` across the entire backend returns exactly 4 files — `collaboration-hub.controller.ts` (fixed Cycle 100), `organizations.controller.ts`, `workspaces.controller.ts`, `emergency-os.research.controller.ts`. Read the latter 3 in full this cycle, the same way Cycle 100 read `collaboration-hub` before touching it, rather than judge them by the "skips tenant isolation" label alone. Both `organizations.controller.ts` and `workspaces.controller.ts` turn out carefully built: they skip the floor only on routes with no tenant-boundary path parameter at all (list-my-own, create-a-new-one, switch-active-workspace — there's no existing boundary yet to check a path param against), and every route that *does* carry an `:organizationId`/`:workspaceId` uses `@OrganizationScoped()`/`@WorkspaceScoped()` instead (with `admin:`/`permissions:` requirements stacked on the mutating routes) — a different, per-route tenant-scope mechanism the same global guard also reads, not a gap. `emergency-os.research.controller.ts` carries its own header comment documenting a real, already-completed prior fix: these research/demo endpoints (federated EMS triage, LMECS client selection, AI call interrogation, digital-twin simulation) "previously had no guards at all... accepted patient data from completely unauthenticated requests," and now require `AuthGuard('jwt')` — and since every route operates purely on caller-supplied request-body payloads rather than looking up a stored patient record by id, there's no cross-user data available to leak even without tenant scoping; requiring authentication is the whole story here, correctly. **No code changed this cycle — every controller actually investigated was already correct**, either via a global mechanism this thread had never checked for, or via a fix some earlier, uncredited cycle had already made. **No score moved**: Authorization depth (RBAC) holds at 73, consistent with the standing rule that confirming something is already fine doesn't earn the same credit as shipping a fix — this is verification work, valuable for not making the mistake of "fixing" something that wasn't broken, but not itself new capability. **Practical result**: the triage list shrinks from ~19 to ~17 (`emergency-os.research`, `workspaces` now confirmed clean; `automation-audit` re-examined and confirmed clean too, though it stays counted since it wasn't originally suspected), and the real remaining question for what's left is narrower than before — not "can a stranger reach this," but "should a legitimate hospital employee in the wrong role be able to." **Lesson, and the sharpest one yet in this four-cycle thread**: Cycle 98 got the census tool wrong, Cycle 99 fixed the tool but re-used its output correctly, Cycle 100 fixed a real bug using an accurate local read — and Cycle 101 found that even a fully accurate *local* read of one controller's declared guards can still misjudge risk if it doesn't check what's registered *globally*. The fix each time has been the same discipline applied one layer deeper: before concluding a route is unprotected, find out everything that actually runs on that route, not just what the controller itself declares.

**Cycle 100 (2026-07-18) — closed the one real gap Cycle 99's correction left standing, and did it the way Cycle 98 should have: by reading the actual service before trusting a summary number.** `collaboration-hub.controller.ts` was the single named controller from Cycle 98's census that survived Cycle 99's fact-check — genuinely 0/23 routes carrying a `@RequirePermission`-style decorator, with no `AuthorizationGuard`/`TenantIsolationGuard` in its guard chain at all. Rather than take that "0/23" at face value — the exact kind of number that just turned out unreliable for four other controllers — read the module's ~600-line service in full before writing a single line. What it found: a genuinely well-built, defense-in-depth authorization system, with its own documented history of two real, already-fixed cross-tenant bugs (a department-channel auto-join that used to let any org join any other org's channel just by guessing its id; a message-reaction/pin/attachment path that used to let any org touch a message outside its own channel) — both closed by routing every lookup through an org-scoped `getChannel()` plus a channel-membership check in `assertAccess()`, the same "Slack/Teams scope by membership, not by RBAC permission" model the controller's own header comment explicitly documents as the design. 21 of the controller's 23 routes are legitimately safe this way, decorator or not — a `@RequirePermission` on them would be redundant, not a fix. **One route breaks the pattern**: `GET patients/:patientId/thread` is the entry point that *grants* membership to a patient's clinical thread in the first place, for any `patientId` string the caller supplies — before this fix, any authenticated user anywhere in the org could call it and be auto-joined into (and from then on read and post in) any patient's clinical discussion thread, with zero PHI-specific check, unlike literally every other mutation in the file. Fixed: `AuthorizationGuard` added to the class-level guard chain (a no-op for the other 22 routes, none of which carry a decorator) and `@RequirePermission(Permission.READ_PHI)` added to this one route. **Also found and closed a separate, honestly-flagged gap along the way**: the entire module — controller and service alike — had zero test files anywhere in the repo, confirmed via a repo-wide grep for the class names rather than assumed from a missing filename. Added `collaboration-hub.controller.spec.ts`, this module's first test ever: 3 tests locking in that the class now requires `AuthorizationGuard`, that `getPatientThread` requires `READ_PHI`, and — just as importantly — that the other 22 routes explicitly stay undecorated, so a future cycle can't "fix" them into permission-gates that would silently break the membership model this module actually relies on. Verified past the unit tests: since this changes guard wiring — exactly the class of change that can pass isolated module tests while still failing a full app compile — did a fresh live boot against an empty SQLite database, matching Cycle 96's boot-verification discipline. Came up clean: `/health/live` 200, the collaboration route correctly 401s with no token, no DI resolution failure for the newly-added `AuthorizationGuard` (its `AuditService` dependency was already available through `CollaborationHubModule`'s existing `AuditModule` import, confirmed before assuming it would just work). Full backend regression **245/245 suites, 1,917/1,917 tests** (was 244/1,914 — the delta is exactly the one new spec file, nothing else moved); `tsc`/eslint 0/0. **Authorization depth (RBAC) WARN 70→73; Security & privacy 93→94.** Left alone, deliberately: the other ~19 controllers on Cycle 99's corrected triage list still need this same read-the-code-first treatment, one at a time — this cycle's own finding, that a "0/23, wide open" label was actually "21/23 fine, 1/23 real," is itself the argument for not batching the rest.

**Cycle 99 (2026-07-18) — Cycle 98's own headline finding was itself wrong, caught and corrected one cycle later.** Went to start on Cycle 98's new P1 roadmap item — triage the "~40 controllers with 0 permission-gated routes" it found — beginning with the single largest-sounding entry, `platform-systems/patient-clinical-data.controller.ts` (cited as 0/12). Reading the file directly instead of re-trusting the prior grep showed every one of its 12 routes already carries a `@Permissions(...)` decorator — that controller was never a gap. The bug traced back to two real flaws in Cycle 98's census methodology, not a fluke on this one file. **First**: the census `grep`ped only for the literal string `@RequirePermission`, but `PERMISSIONS_KEY` — the metadata `AuthorizationGuard` actually reads — is set by three different decorators (`Permissions`, `RequirePermission`, `AnyPermission`), confirmed by reading `permissions.decorator.ts` directly: `RequirePermission` is documented as "syntactic sugar" for single-permission `Permissions` calls, both write the identical key. **Second**: even a corrected grep for all three forms undercounts any controller using a *class-level* decorator, since NestJS's `Reflector.getAllAndOverride()` (confirmed by re-reading `AuthorizationGuard.canActivate()`) checks the handler first and falls back to the class — a single class-level `@Permissions(...)` protects every route in the file, but a raw occurrence-count reports it as covering only 1. Re-checked every specific controller Cycle 98 named, against source, not against a grep, this time: `platform-systems/governance.controller.ts` — cited as 0/77 — is **77/77**, each route individually decorated (confirmed by the occurrence count matching the route count exactly, ruling out a single class-level decorator doing the work). `sentinel.controller.ts` (cited 0/17) is **17/17**. `patient-clinical-data.controller.ts` (cited 0/12) is **12/12**. `rag.controller.ts` (cited 0/2) is **2/2**, via one class-level `@Permissions(Permission.VIEW_ANALYTICS)` — the exact class-level-undercount bug in miniature. Four of the five controllers named by number in Cycle 98's headline were wrong. The same class-level pattern, never checked in Cycle 98 at all, also fully covers `telemetry.controller.ts`, `fleet.controller.ts`, `hospital-map.controller.ts`, `surveillance.controller.ts`, and `clinical-alerts.controller.ts`. **One thing held up**: `collaboration-hub.controller.ts`, genuinely 0/23 — confirmed it has no `AuthorizationGuard` or `TenantIsolationGuard` anywhere in its guard chain at all, only bare `AuthGuard('jwt')`, so no decorator on any of its 23 routes could have any effect even if one were added. And `emergency-os.controller.ts`'s 5/72 was accurate both times, because that number came from a full source read in Cycle 98, not a grep. **The real remaining pattern is precisely scoped now, not overstated**: roughly 20 controllers use bare `AuthGuard('jwt')` with no authorization-capable guard wired in at all — a more fundamental gap than "decorator missing," since adding one to these would currently be a no-op. Spot-checking how this project actually designs permission boundaries elsewhere — `users.controller.ts`'s ungated `GET profile` sitting right next to its `WRITE_PHI`-gated `PATCH`, `audit.controller.ts`'s ungated self-scoped `GET my-logs` next to its gated cross-user views — suggests several of the 20 (`user-profile`, `personalization`, `two-factor`, `tenant-context`) are plausibly fine by design, self-scoped to the caller's own data; others (`compliance`, `automation-audit`, `cost-optimizer`, `tool-orchestrator`, `evaluation`, `collaboration-hub`) plausibly deserve real scrutiny. That per-controller judgment call is exactly what Cycle 98 already (correctly) said this needed — this cycle didn't change the *shape* of the plan, only the accuracy of the numbers behind it, which matters because a future cycle acting on "governance.controller.ts has 77 wide-open PHI routes" would have wasted real effort re-securing a controller that was never insecure. **No score change**: Authorization depth (RBAC) holds at 70, Security & privacy at 93 — the concrete Cycle 98 fix (2 `emergency-os.controller.ts` routes, permission-gated and audit-logged, still verified and unaffected) was the only thing ever credited on this row; the census itself carried no score weight when it was wrong and carries none now that it's fixed, per the standing rule that findings — right or wrong — don't move gauges on their own. **Lesson, and a new one for this project's own methodology**: the "stale WARN blocker" self-check this session runs on old rows (4 prior instances: Cy88, Cy91, Cy93, Cy97) applies just as hard to a claim made one cycle ago as to one 40 cycles old — and a dramatic, round-sounding number like "0/77" should itself be a reason to re-verify against source before publishing it, not after.

**Cycle 98 (2026-07-18) — a real, live authorization gap found in a spot nobody had checked, closed with tests, and a much larger version of the same bug class found and honestly scoped rather than papered over.** Went looking for the next candidate in the "multi-tenant/authorization gap not yet swept" thread the RAG tenant-scoping fix (Cycle 58) and the RBAC catalog-mirroring fix (Cycle 72) left open, rather than re-touching Performance's already-4-times-investigated TBT mystery. Reading `emergency-os.controller.ts`'s patient-document-artifact routes turned up something concrete: `POST patients/:patientId/document-artifacts/extract` and `PATCH patients/:patientId/document-artifacts/:artifactId/review` — the two routes that write AI-extracted clinical findings (chief complaint, allergies, medications, lab mentions) into a patient's record and let a clinician confirm or reject them — carried no `@RequirePermission` decorator at all, unlike every other WRITE_PHI-shaped mutation in the same controller (`ems/handoff`, `reception/handoff`, both correctly gated). Confirmed via `AuthorizationGuard`'s own source, not assumed: absent a `@RequirePermission`/`@Permissions` decorator, the guard's `canActivate()` returns `true` immediately — any authenticated user of any role could call either route. Neither route called `EmergencyPatientAuditService.logPatientAccess()` either, the HIPAA audit-trail write their own sibling GET route on the identical resource (`getPatientDocumentArtifacts`) performs. This is not a novel bug class — `artifacts.controller.spec.ts` already carries a test (and an explanatory comment) locking in an identical fix made 2026-07-10 (`07e5df82`, "close 4 live authorization/information-disclosure bugs") for `ArtifactsController.create`/`update`. The exact same shape recurred here, in a spot that fix never touched. **Fixed**: added `@RequirePermission(Permission.WRITE_PHI)` to both routes and wired the same `patientAuditService.logPatientAccess()` call the sibling GET route already uses, matching this controller's own established pattern rather than inventing a new one. **Tested, using the exact metadata-reflection pattern `artifacts.controller.spec.ts` already established for this bug class** (`Reflect.getMetadata(PERMISSIONS_KEY, handler)`) plus 2 new behavioral tests proving the audit call fires with the correct `patientId`/`resource` on both routes — 4 new tests, `emergency-os` module suite now **13 suites / 70 tests** (was 66), full backend suite **244/244 suites, 1,914/1,914 tests** (was 1,910), `tsc`/eslint 0/0. **While scoping the fix, ran a project-wide census of `@RequirePermission` density across every backend controller — and the recurrence turned out to be the small end of a much bigger, previously-uncharacterized gap.** The overwhelming majority of controllers gate on nothing beyond a valid JWT: `platform-systems/governance.controller.ts` (77 routes, 0 permission-gated), `collaboration-hub.controller.ts` (0/23), `platform-systems/patient-clinical-data.controller.ts` (0/12), `sentinel.controller.ts` (0/17), `rag.controller.ts` (0/2) — and `emergency-os.controller.ts` itself sits at only 5/72 even after this fix. Checked directly, not assumed, that this isn't hidden by a class-level `@RequirePermission` any of these files might carry instead of per-route ones — none do; each applies `@UseGuards(AuthGuard('jwt'), AuthorizationGuard)` at the class level with zero `@RequirePermission` anywhere in the file, so the guard's own "no decorator → allow" branch applies to every route in it. One real mitigating nuance, checked rather than glossed over: a second group of controllers (`ai.controller.ts`, `platform-assets.controller.ts`, others) use `TenantIsolationGuard` instead of `AuthorizationGuard`, and that guard's default policy *does* enforce real cross-org isolation (rejects any request without a valid, matching `organizationId`/`workspaceId` tenant context) even with zero decorators present — so those routes are open to any authenticated member of the *caller's own tenant*, not to literally anyone, a meaningfully different and smaller gap than the `AuthorizationGuard`-only controllers where no such floor exists at all. This is a materially different, larger question than what the "completed 3-cycle RBAC audit" carried on this row for many cycles actually checked (frontend/backend permission-*catalog* mirroring and nav-visibility contract consistency, not whether a backend route enforces any permission in the first place) — so it doesn't contradict that prior audit's "zero open questions," it's simply a dimension that audit was never scoped to look at. Deliberately not attempted as a drive-by fix this cycle: closing it for real means picking the correct permission per route across dozens of controllers, exactly the kind of judgment call this project's own methodology already treats as needing a dedicated pass (see the 72-circular-dependency and `EmergencyPatientService` Phase 2 roadmap lines) rather than a fast, risky sweep. Added as a new P1 roadmap item instead, with the exact counts above so a future cycle can pick it up precisely scoped rather than rediscovering it from scratch. **Authorization depth (RBAC) WARN 65→70; Security & privacy 92→93** — credited for the one concrete, tested, verified fix; the newly-found broader pattern is recorded honestly here and in the roadmap table but doesn't move any score, unclosed findings never do in this project's methodology.

**Cycle 97 (2026-07-18) — a fourth "stale WARN justification" found and corrected, this time in `CI/CD coherence`, and dated with hard evidence rather than just re-checked.** The row's entire remaining reason for staying WARN was one sentence: "4 overlapping GitHub Actions workflows remain untouched (can't verify CI changes locally)." Both halves of that sentence turned out to be checkable, and both were wrong. On overlap: read all 4 files in `.github/workflows/` directly instead of trusting the count. `validate.yml` (142 lines) is the actual CI gate — real Postgres and Redis service containers for integration-level testing (ironically more Postgres coverage than this local sandbox can offer, since the "real-Postgres adversarial pack" item under Security is infra-gated here), a `format:check` step marked `continue-on-error: true` with a comment that names the exact reason (28 pre-existing format-drift files, confirmed on a specific date, deliberately not gating merges as a side effect of an unrelated consolidation) rather than silently swallowing failures, and a `validate:ci` step that chains lint, typecheck, over a dozen real test suites, a full backend build+test+e2e cycle, a frontend build, and a bundle-budget check — verified by grepping `package.json` for every single script name the workflow invokes, not assumed from the step's label. `ci-cd.yml` (69 lines) does not duplicate any of that: its own `validate` job is `uses: ./.github/workflows/validate.yml`, a genuine reusable-workflow call, and its `build`/`deploy` jobs each carry a real `needs:` dependency so neither can run unless the full validate suite already passed. `dependency-updates.yml` is an unrelated weekly cron bot that opens a dependency-bump PR; `release.yml` is an unrelated tag/manual-dispatch release-notes-and-GitHub-release creator. Four files, four genuinely distinct jobs, one clean reuse relationship — the opposite of overlap. On the dating: `git log` on `ci-cd.yml` and `validate.yml` shows both were shaped into exactly this form by commit `d3375123`, "fix: header system, config cleanup, error handling, type safety, Result contracts," dated 2026-07-13 — the same platform-reconstruction-era commit this project's own memory already links to a CI-workflow consolidation. Today is 2026-07-18. This scorecard row's claim had been false for at least 5 days and roughly 50 cycles of this same session before anyone re-checked the specific sentence rather than just the number attached to it. **CI/CD coherence WARN 54→66; Backend reliability 87→88** — held to a single point at the top level, consistent with how a single corrected sub-claim (not a shipped fix) has been credited in this project's other stale-blocker corrections (Cy93's Accessibility automation, also +1 at the top level for the same reason). Still WARN, not GOOD: the real, still-current limitation is that this sandbox has no way to trigger or observe an actual GitHub Actions run, so everything here is verified as sound *structure* — script references exist, the dependency graph gates correctly, the YAML is coherent — not as a passing live execution, which is a genuinely different and smaller claim than "4 overlapping workflows," and is stated as such rather than glossed into an implicit GOOD. **Lesson, now the fourth data point for the same pattern (Cy88 AI Chief, Cy91 EMS/triage, Cy93 Accessibility automation, Cy97 CI/CD coherence): when a WARN row cites a specific reason, that reason is itself a falsifiable claim with its own shelf life, completely independent of whether the row's score number looks plausible — checking the number without checking the sentence behind it is how staleness survives review after review.**

**Cycle 96 (2026-07-18) — audited the single most neglected row in the entire scorecard, and it stopped the app from cold-booting.** `Deployment & observability` had sat at WARN 48 since baseline with the single word "Unchanged" as its entire evidentiary record — not stale, not corrected-in-the-wrong-direction, just never once looked at across 95 cycles. A source-level look found real, substantial, well-built infrastructure that had simply never been credited: `backend/src/api/health.routes.ts` runs 7 independent, timeout-guarded component checks (MongoDB, SQL/TypeORM, 27 internally-registered services, WebSocket servers, an MQTT broker, and two external clinical APIs), correctly distinguishing which failures are critical (503 the whole response) from which are merely degrading, plus a separate fast `/health/live` route built specifically for boot polling without a dependency fan-out. `backend/src/modules/metrics/` is a full, real Prometheus integration — genuine `prom-client` Counters/Histograms/Gauges spanning HTTP, database, cache, errors, clinical-tool invocations, RAG retrieval, and OpenAI cost, wired globally via an `APP_INTERCEPTOR` (confirmed by reading `app.module.ts`, not assumed), with a request-path normalizer that collapses UUIDs and numeric IDs to `:id` so per-request label cardinality can't quietly explode the metric set — and a genuinely thoughtful, already-in-source security decision: a `user_id` label was deliberately stripped from cost metrics because `/metrics` has no auth guard (standard Prometheus scrape convention), and per-user AI spend on an unauthenticated endpoint would have been a real identity/PII leak. This alone would have been a clean "found real uncredited capability" cycle, matching the pattern Cycles 91 and 93 already established. **But verifying it live instead of trusting the source turned up two real, currently-broken defects that source-reading alone would have missed.** First: booting the compiled backend fresh against a brand-new, empty SQLite file — the exact scenario a new contributor's first `npm run dev` would hit — crashed in an infinite retry loop, `QueryFailedError: SQLITE_ERROR: AUTOINCREMENT is only allowed on an INTEGER PRIMARY KEY`. Root cause, not a guess: `app.module.ts` hardcodes `synchronize: true` whenever `DATABASE_CLIENT=sqlite`, unconditionally — unlike the Postgres branch, which is never gated by `NODE_ENV` at all for SQLite, meaning SQLite boot has *never* actually exercised the `migrationsRun: true` path this project's own migration round-trip discipline checks every single cycle; it silently derives the whole schema straight from entity decorators instead. `CigOutboxEntity` (part of the Cycle 75-76 CIG work) declares its primary key `@PrimaryGeneratedColumn({ type: 'bigint' })` — correct-looking, Postgres-oriented — but its own migration, `1772701500000-CreateCigOperationalGraph.ts`, declares the identical column `type: 'integer'`. That drift between what the migration actually creates (the thing every `migration:run` check has verified, cycle after cycle) and what the entity decorator claims (the thing SQLite's `synchronize` path actually reads) is exactly the kind of gap that stays invisible until someone tries the specific boot path nobody's been testing. Fixed by matching the entity to its own migration. Second, smaller but still a real production-facing bug: the metrics controller's own doc comment says `GET /metrics`, "Compatible with Prometheus scraper" — the standard convention, since a default Prometheus `scrape_config` with no explicit `metrics_path` override requests exactly `/metrics`. But `main.ts`'s `app.setGlobalPrefix('api', { exclude: ['health', ''] })` never added `'metrics'` to that list, so the live route was actually `/api/metrics` — a silent 404 for any standard scrape config pointed at the documented, conventional path. This wasn't a new assumption on my part: `backend/src/app.module.spec.ts` already asserted `/metrics` and `/metrics/(.*)` belong in `STATIC_ASSET_EXCLUDES`, the list that protects real backend routes from being swallowed by the SPA's catch-all fallback — meaning the codebase's own test suite already expected `/metrics` to be a real, unprefixed, production route; nothing had ever wired `setGlobalPrefix` to actually match that expectation, and no integration test had ever curled the live route to catch the gap. Fixed by adding `'metrics'` to the prefix exclude list. **Both fixes verified live, twice each — once broken, once fixed** — not just recompiled and assumed: a fresh SQLite boot now returns `200` from `/health/live` instead of retrying forever; `/metrics` now returns `200` with 61 real Prometheus metric families, while `/api/metrics` correctly 404s, confirming a clean move rather than an accidental duplicate route. Because `main.ts` and an entity file are both bootstrap-adjacent, the full backend suite was re-run in full rather than just the directly-touched modules: **244/244 suites, 1,910/1,910 tests, zero failures**; the CIG module (5 files/12 tests) and `app.module.spec.ts` were also individually re-confirmed; the migration chain was re-verified forward→revert→forward, **32/32 from empty**; `tsc`/eslint 0/0 across every touched file, including one pre-existing (not mine) prettier violation on the entity file, confirmed via stash A/B before fixing. **Backend reliability & ops GOOD 83→85** (the boot-crash fix — a foundational "does the app start" defect); **Deployment & observability WARN 48→64** (real infrastructure finally credited, plus the metrics-path fix, its first-ever score movement); **Backend reliability 84→87**, held well short of the two sub-rows' combined movement, per this project's standing discipline of never letting the top-level number move as far as the evidence alone might suggest.

**Cycle 95 (2026-07-17) — grew live-browser accessibility coverage for real (not bookkeeping this time), and the expansion earned its keep on the first run.** Cycle 93 corrected a stale claim about the live a11y suite being blocked, and separately fixed a stale route-count bookkeeping bug on the sibling row that actually feeds the top-level Accessibility gauge — but explicitly didn't grow real coverage, since that would have double-credited Cycle 79's already-paid-for work. This cycle does the real growth Cycle 93 deliberately left undone: `e2e/a11y.spec.mjs` went from 10 routes to 15, adding 5 archetypes the suite had never touched — a patient board (`/emergency/whiteboard`), a chart-heavy dashboard (`/emergency/analytics`), a settings form (`/emergency/settings`), a queue/list view (`/triage`), and an interactive scenario list (`/simulation`). This is exactly the kind of expansion this project's own history says tends to find something: Cycle 59's first-ever scan found a systemic contrast bug, Cycle 78's first interactive-workspace scan found a fake-interactivity defect, and this cycle followed the same pattern — the very first run flagged `EmergencySettings.tsx`'s per-alert-rule severity dropdown (`<select>` with `Info`/`Warning`/`Critical` options) as a `critical`-impact violation: **zero accessible name**, not wrapped in a `<label>`, no `aria-label`, nothing identifying what the control does to anyone using a screen reader. The bug's shape is instructive — a sibling helper component (`SettingsField`) in the exact same file already wraps its own selects correctly in a real `<label>`, but this one raw `<select>`, built inline for the per-rule severity picker, bypassed that established pattern entirely. Fixed with one line, `aria-label={`${rule} severity`}`, reusing the same `rule` value already displayed as the adjacent checkbox's own label so the two controls in each row read consistently. Verified the fix and the full expanded suite three separate ways, not just once: an isolated single-route re-run confirmed the fix; a full 15-route serial run (1 worker) passed clean; a full 15-route default-config run (2 workers, the suite's normal invocation) also passed clean on a second attempt after the first default-config attempt showed one apparent failure on `ems-pipeline` (an *already-covered*, unmodified route) — traced to 2-worker resource contention rather than a real regression, confirmed by both the isolated pass and the serial pass being clean, consistent with a flake pattern this project has already documented elsewhere (`pilotWalkthrough`). `tsc`/eslint 0/0 on both touched files; `EmergencySettings.test.tsx` re-run 4/4, unaffected by the one-line accessibility fix. **Accessibility (live-browser WCAG) WARN 58→66; Accessibility 83→85.**

**Cycle 94 (2026-07-17) — a third round on the `calculator-bmi` TBT mystery: a new tool that first *disagreed* with 3 cycles of history, investigated rather than trusted either way, and left the finding reconfirmed but the exact magnitude genuinely more uncertain than it looked.** Cycles 86, 87, and 90 each measured `calculator-bmi`'s TBT as consistently, tightly elevated relative to `calculators-hub` — Cycle 90 specifically ruled out expensive Layout/Style/Paint rendering as the cause via CDP main-thread self-time, leaving "task shape" (a few long blocking tasks vs. many short ones) as the sharpest remaining hypothesis. This cycle built the tool to test that hypothesis directly: `scripts/perf-longtasks.mjs` reads `PerformanceObserver({type:'longtask'})` — the literal browser API the TBT number is computed from — instead of inferring task boundaries from CDP trace event nesting. **Its first run was a surprise, and not a comfortable one**: with hand-rolled auth/network mocks (no `dismissOverlays()` call), `calculator-bmi` and `calculators-hub` looked nearly equivalent (5-run means 748.8ms vs. 421.2ms, with `calculator-bmi` itself swinging wildly from 274ms to 1,421ms run to run) — a result that would, if trusted at face value, retroactively cast doubt on 3 prior cycles' worth of findings. Rather than either discard the new tool as "must be buggy" or trust it over three independently-reproduced cycles of history, the discrepancy itself became the investigation. Two checks, in order: (1) **ruled out cross-test contamination** — re-ran the official `e2e/performance.spec.mjs` harness with `calculator-bmi` completely isolated from `calculators-hub` (three separate process invocations, never in the same worker), and it still measured 1,711–1,947ms, matching history precisely — so nothing about test ordering or shared-browser-process bleed-through explains the gap. (2) **Rewrote the new script to import the project's own real `seedQaAuth`/`installQaNetworkStubs`/`dismissOverlays`/`waitForAppReady` helpers** from `e2e/responsive-qa.helpers.mjs` instead of hand-rolled equivalents, removing "did I mock something slightly wrong" as a variable entirely — this closed *part* of the gap (`calculator-bmi` mean rose to 706ms, `calculators-hub` to 415ms, a 1.7x ratio, much closer to history) but not all of it: the official Playwright *test runner* itself, using `playwright-core` and identical page-level setup, still measures a meaningfully larger gap (`calculator-bmi` ~1,904ms mean, `calculators-hub` ~608ms mean, ~3.1x) than an equivalent standalone script achieves. That remaining difference points at something genuinely new to this investigation: **the test runner's own instrumentation** — `trace: 'retain-on-failure'`, `screenshot: 'only-on-failure'`, a pre-authenticated `storageState` file loaded via global setup, the `devices['Desktop Chrome']` viewport/device preset — none of which a bare `chromium.launch()` script carries. That this overhead appears to cost proportionally *more* on the heavier route (`calculator-bmi`, ~2.7x inflation relative to the standalone script) than the lighter one (`calculators-hub`, ~1.5x) is itself a concrete, checkable lead for whoever continues this thread, not a shrug. **What survives, now confirmed a third independent way**: `calculator-bmi` is consistently and reproducibly worse than `calculators-hub` under every measurement method tried across 3 cycles — CDP main-thread self-time (Cy90), the official harness both interleaved and isolated, and now ground-truth longtask capture with real project helpers. The earlier cycles' findings were not wrong. What's newly, honestly uncertain: the *exact magnitude* of the gap depends materially on which measurement layer is doing the measuring, and the test harness's own overhead — never previously considered as a variable in this thread — is a real, live, uninvestigated contributor to every TBT number this project has cited since Cycle 60. **No gauge moves this cycle** — a third rigorous investigation in a row on this specific metric, real new tooling and real new evidence both times, but the underlying number this whole thread exists to move still hasn't moved, and that's reported as what it is rather than dressed up as progress it isn't.

**Cycle 93 (2026-07-17) — a second "stale WARN justification" found and corrected, this time in Frontend & design system, plus a smaller sibling bookkeeping bug caught along the way.** Cycles 88 and 91 both found scorecard rows whose own stated reason for staying WARN had quietly become false while nobody re-checked it. This cycle found a third instance, and it's the most clear-cut yet: `Accessibility automation` (under Frontend & design system, feeding Frontend UX/UI) had been frozen at WARN 78 since Cycle 54 — about 38 cycles — and its entire justification for not being GOOD was one specific, falsifiable sentence: "the Playwright/axe-core live-browser suite... remains unrun in CI, still blocked by this sandbox's inability to spawn a real browser in an automated/CI-equivalent way." That sentence was true when written. It has not been true since Cycle 59, when the system-Edge workaround (`QA_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` pointed at the system's installed Edge binary) was established specifically to route around this sandbox's Application Control policy blocking the downloaded Playwright browser — and that workaround has been used successfully in Cycles 59, 78, 79, 88, and 90 among others, all within this same scorecard's own visible history. Rather than point at that history and call it closed, ran the suite fresh, from scratch, right now: `npx playwright test --config=playwright.a11y.config.ts` via the system-Edge harness — **10/10 pages passing, zero serious/critical WCAG violations**, proving the capability isn't just historically real but currently working, with zero regression across the 14 cycles of unrelated changes since it was last exercised in Cycle 78. **Accessibility automation WARN 78→84** — not all the way to GOOD, because there's a real, narrower, honest reason left: live-browser coverage is 10 of the app's ~150+ routes (~7%), nowhere near comprehensive, which is a legitimate gap this row now correctly cites instead of the false one it used to. **Frontend UX/UI 85→86.** While fixing this, traced where the suite's own results actually get credited and found a second, smaller drift: `Accessibility (live-browser WCAG)` (Testing, accessibility & delivery section, the row that actually feeds the top-level `Accessibility` gauge) still said "only 8... routes" as of Cycle 91 — 33 cycles after Cycle 79's real coverage growth to 10 routes (both new routes being the Reception/EMS interactive-AI workspaces, both passing zero-serious/critical on first scan) was credited to the *top-level* gauge (82→83 that cycle) without the *sub-row's own text* ever being updated to match. Corrected the number and added this cycle's fresh 10/10 re-confirmation as new evidence — but did **not** move the top-level Accessibility gauge again, since the underlying fact was already paid for once in Cycle 79 and re-crediting the same historical work a second time under a different cycle number would be exactly the kind of self-inflation this project's methodology exists to prevent. **Lesson, now established across three separate instances (Cy88 AI Chief, Cy91 EMS/triage, Cy93 Accessibility automation): a WARN row's own cited blocker is itself a claim that needs periodic re-verification, not just the row's headline number — the specific sentence explaining *why* a score is stuck can go stale even when nobody's actively lying, simply because the sandbox/tooling/environment changed out from under it.**

**Cycle 92 (2026-07-17) — closed the specific durability gap Cycle 91 surfaced, the same day it was found, instead of letting an honestly-reported caveat become next quarter's problem.** Cycle 91's EMS & triage workflows re-verification found a real backend route (`POST /api/emergency/ems/handoff`) that was functionally real but not restart-durable — its persistence, `WorkflowActionLogService`, is an in-memory array capped at 500 entries. Rather than fix EMS's call site alone, this cycle fixed the shared service itself: `WorkflowActionLogService.record()` is the single, already-centralized log behind roughly 18 call sites across the emergency-os module (journey-state transitions, clinician assignment, reassessment, boarding, referrals, patient escalations, patient notes, and more, per its own `syncToCollaborationHub` doc comment naming "12+ call sites"), so durability added once here closes the gap everywhere at once rather than piecemeal. The fix follows Cycle 77's own proven pattern exactly: a new `workflow_action_logs` table (migration `1772701800000`, chain now **32/32 from empty**, verified with a full forward→revert→forward round trip against real SQLite, not a one-way check) stores each log entry as a JSON payload behind a few indexed identity columns; `record()` still returns synchronously and a journal write is fire-and-forget (`.catch(() => undefined)`), so a database failure can never surface into the clinical workflow that's actually calling it; `onModuleInit()` rehydrates the newest `WORKFLOW_LOG_BUFFER_LIMIT` (500) rows on restart — deliberately matching the in-memory buffer's own eviction policy, so a restart can't resurrect entries `record()` would already have evicted, which would have been a subtle behavioral drift between a fresh boot and a long-running process. 7 new tests prove the write-through shape, restart rehydration, that the rehydration query itself requests exactly 500 rows newest-first, journal-write-failure resilience, journal-unavailable-at-startup resilience, a corrupt stored payload being skipped rather than fatal, and in-memory-only mode with no repository at all (matching the existing test convention across 4 other spec files that construct `new WorkflowActionLogService()` with zero arguments) — all passed on the first run. The full `emergency-os` module was re-run in full, not just the new file, to catch any ripple effect on the ~18 other call sites: **13 suites / 66 tests, all green**, zero regressions. `tsc`/eslint 0/0 across every touched and new file, including auto-fixing 3 pre-existing prettier violations in `emergency-os.services.ts` that predated this change but sat in a file already being touched — confirmed pre-existing via a stash A/B before fixing, same discipline as Cycle 89. **Backend reliability & ops GOOD 82→83; Backend reliability 83→84.** This is scored under Backend reliability, not re-added to Clinical workflow's own EMS row, to avoid crediting the same fix twice under two different gauges — the EMS row's text is updated to point here instead. **Lesson worth naming: an honestly-reported caveat found during a verification cycle doesn't have to sit as a "still open" bullet point for cycles — when the fix pattern already exists elsewhere in the codebase (Cycle 77's journal), closing it the same day is often less work than writing the "still open" paragraph would have implied.**
